C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?D5821st of 41full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F4037th of 41

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Route to market and intermediaries: helping or hindering, and how fragile?

Judgement: Mildly hindering and fragile. SKAN sells directly with its own sales and service organisations (Switzerland, Germany, Brazil, Japan, the US), which helps service capture; but for full fill-finish lines it reaches customers through partnerships with filling-machine makers (Groninger, Bausch+Ströbel and others) who are simultaneously building their own isolators. These "frenemy" partners are the intermediaries that matter, and their incentive is to keep more of the line value for themselves.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • Former CEO Huber acknowledged losing orders to Syntegon because SKAN did not offer integrated lines; SKAN answered with partner integrations rather than its own filler, which the Leadership report calls adaptability "partial".
  • The Optima expert: in some bids "nobody is offering third-party SKAN isolators".
  • Engineering and EPCM firms and customers' validation teams also specify suppliers; SKAN's regulatory reference status (inspector training, ISPE guides) helps here.
  • Delivered-at-Place terms and US-bound production in Görlitz keep tariffs off SKAN's books.

Modelling implication: The fragility is non-linear: one partner changing its policy could move several per cent of E&S intake within a year. It is not a base-case driver for 2026 to 2027 because the backlog is already secured.

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

F3638th of 41

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Existing competitors: their behaviour, its effect on SKAN, and SKAN's fragility against them

Judgement: Existing competitors' behaviour is hurting SKAN at the margin: filler OEMs are internalising isolators and bundling, Syntegon is investing in gloveless high-speed isolators and North American service, Getinge is pushing ISOPRIME and ISOFLEX isolators and sterile transfer bags, and Esco is winning sterility-testing isolators in North America on price. SKAN's position is robust in complex lines and fragile in standard ones.

Reasoning: Competitor actions are rational responses to a market where the isolator is becoming a larger share of line value (Annex 1, higher complexity). They target the profit pool SKAN has owned. SKAN's defences are technology (eBeam, containment, robotics, digital), reference status with regulators, partner networks and the installed-base service relationship.

C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?A723rd of 41full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

D4627th of 41

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting, and how fragile do they leave SKAN?

Judgement: Disruption acts on four actors, and SKAN's net position is moderately fragile. (1) Customers: drug-pricing pressure, GLP-1 moderation, capex scrutiny and dual-sourcing lengthen decisions and make buyers more price-conscious. (2) Competitors: line builders are rebundling the line and moving into isolators. (3) Regulators: tightening sterility rules raise the value of the isolator. (4) Suppliers: tariffs and sanctions documentation add cost and administration but not scarcity. The value chain is shifting the profit pool toward validated consumables and mandatory service (Researcher, 1-Nov-26), away from the equipment stage, where SKAN earns about 65% of revenue.

Reasoning: Within the value chain, SKAN sits at the equipment stage, which earns thin margins: E&S EBITDA of 4.3% in 2025 against upstream component suppliers (West Pharma about 43% segment gross margin, Aptar Pharma 32% to 36% EBITDA) and downstream contract manufacturers (Lonza about 35% core EBITDA). SKAN's strategy (S&C to 50%, consumables, software, PAS) is the correct response, but it is being executed while the equipment stage is under more pressure.

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

C6815th of 41

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Market rank and distance from number one or two

Judgement: SKAN is number one globally in isolators for aseptic and aseptic-toxic drug manufacturing, with an estimated 30% to 40% share (management, FY 2024), about 35% of the premium high-value aseptic isolator segment and about 25% of the overall high-value isolator market (Business Foundations), and about 50% of process isolators on fill-finish lines (Optima expert, down from about 70% in 2023). It is the clear leader and the only large independent isolator specialist. Measured against the whole fill-finish line market, however, SKAN is a mid-sized specialist: its 2025 sales of CHF 333m compare with Syntegon's EUR 1.75bn and Optima's EUR 850m.

Reasoning: The isolator lead is real and reinforced by an installed base of about 1,100 to 1,150 isolators and about 50 eBeam decontamination systems. The relevant competitive unit, however, is shifting toward the full line, where SKAN's partners (Groninger, Bausch+Ströbel) and rivals (Syntegon, Optima, IMA) control the filler and increasingly the isolator too. Number one in a component whose buyers are consolidating purchases into packages is a weaker position than number one in a standalone product.

Modelling implication: Base the E&S revenue model on a stable-to-slightly-falling share of a growing isolator market (market growth 8% to 10%), not on a defended 50%+ share.

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

C-6418th of 41

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Market concentration and the steepness of the share distribution

Judgement: The isolator market is highly concentrated: SKAN alone holds 30% to 40%, so the top five comfortably exceed 30% (very likely 60% or more). The share distribution is steep at the top (SKAN several times the next player) with a long tail of regional and Chinese makers. The broader liquid-filling equipment sector is described as "highly concentrated, dominated by a few engineering specialists", clustered in Germany (Syntegon, Optima, Groninger, Bausch+Ströbel) plus IMA (Italy).

Reasoning: The files do not give shares for numbers two to ten, so the five-times test cannot be computed precisely. The qualitative evidence (one dominant independent specialist, a handful of large filler OEMs with in-house isolators, then Getinge, Comecer, Franz Ziel, Esco, Tofflon and others) points to a distribution where the top five hold more than five times the share of numbers six to ten. Concentration supports price discipline in complex lines; it does not prevent tender competition in standard lines.

Modelling implication: Concentration is supportive, but the relevant concentration for pricing is at the line level, where four to five German and Italian groups bid against each other in tenders.

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

F3638th of 41

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Share stability and new entrants' share over three to five years

Judgement: Shares are not stable in process isolators. The best available evidence (a competitor's estimate, so biased upward) is that SKAN lost about 20 points of process-isolator share in two years, to filling-machine makers that developed their own isolators, and that experts expect a further gradual decline over five years. These gainers are adjacent incumbents rather than new companies. In complex niches (eBeam high-speed tub lines, aseptic-toxic ADC lines, cell and gene therapy isolators) and in laboratory isolators the files show no share loss, except Esco gaining in standard sterility-testing isolators in North America.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • The share loss coincides with the shift to turnkey procurement (customers want one purchase order and one project manager) and with the post-pandemic shift from allocation to tender markets.
  • Management's counter-evidence: it won most of the larger US fill-finish projects in H1 2026 and says it defended its global position against Optima in recent tenders; its 30% to 40% global figure covers all isolators.
  • Reconciliation: SKAN is likely losing share in standard and mid-complexity lines and holding share in complex lines. That is consistent with rising average order values and a falling count of standard process isolator wins.

Modelling implication: Assume SKAN's isolator share drifts down by 1 to 3 points a year in process isolators through 2029, while its revenue grows with the complex end of the market.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

F4038th of 41

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Does share consolidate toward the largest or the most advantaged, and is SKAN among them?

Judgement: Share is consolidating toward the line integrators (the largest players by revenue), because buyers prefer single-source accountability for complex installations: Syntegon bought Telstar to offer freeze-drying plus isolation and is the clearest consolidator. In the complex, high-specification end, share consolidates toward the most technically advantaged, and there SKAN is among the leaders (eBeam, aseptic-toxic containment, regulatory reference status). SKAN is therefore among the advantaged in its niche but not among the consolidators of the market as a whole.

Reasoning: The two forces pull in opposite directions. Customer demand for turnkey lines favours scale bundlers; Annex 1 complexity and toxic-drug containment favour the specialist. Some buyers reject single-source lines and prefer best-of-breed combinations; a former Syntegon director believes integration and automation are the key to advantage; SKAN's former CEO said Syntegon's integration "adapted to the lowest level of quality". The outcome over five years probably depends on whether SKAN's partnership model (Groninger, Bausch+Ströbel) can offer one accountable project lead.

Modelling implication: Keep a scenario in which a filler partner (for example Bausch+Ströbel, which already has its own isolators) stops proposing SKAN in joint bids; this would take a meaningful slice of standard-line orders.

C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

D4631st of 41

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Competitors' attitude: margin first or volume first; do prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: Mostly margin-and-quality first, but with a clear exception developing. The German filling specialists (Groninger, Bausch+Ströbel, Optima) are family-owned engineering firms that compete on quality and reliability rather than price wars; Syntegon is private-equity owned (CVC with Apollo as a 37% minority from March 2026) and is growing EBITDA aggressively (EBITDA +27% to EUR 282m in 2025, a 16.1% margin; nine-month adjusted EBITDA +40% in 2025), so it seeks both volume and margin. Chinese makers (Tofflon) are price-first and face a weak domestic market. Prices in equipment did soften when volume fell: the 2023 to 2025 order slump turned an allocation market into a tender market where SKAN must "fight much more to win".

Reasoning: The softening appears in the form of pricing parity among the main European suppliers driven by price-conscious contract manufacturers and biosimilar makers, rather than in overt discounting. No competitor is described as willing to suffer losses to win share.

Modelling implication: In a renewed downturn, assume equipment pricing falls modestly (low-to-mid single digits on new orders) and order win rates fall, not a price war.

C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

D4230th of 41

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Stability of that attitude, and the risk of a "shark attack" on pricing

Judgement: Moderately stable over the last eighteen months to three years, with two changes that raise the risk of opportunistic pricing: Syntegon's new capital from Apollo, earmarked for service and North American expansion, and Chinese makers' export push into price-sensitive segments while their home market is weak. The German family firms show little "O'Leary" instability. A shark attack on industry pricing is plausible in standard lines and contract-manufacturer tenders if SKAN were seen as weakened (for example a CFO crisis or another delivery failure), but not in complex aseptic-toxic and eBeam lines where qualification limits the number of credible bidders.

Reasoning: The trigger for opportunistic pricing would be a capex plateau in 2028 (fewer projects, same capacity). The industry added capacity during the pandemic boom (SKAN added more than 200 specialists in 2023 alone); a digestion phase would test discipline.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

D5030th of 41

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Moderately supportive and moderately fragile. Supportive: a concentrated isolator market, high technical and regulatory barriers (validation, Annex 1, toxic containment), a regulation-forced demand tailwind, and buyers who cannot terminally sterilise toxic drugs and therefore must buy sealed isolators. Fragile: the line-level structure is shifting power to bundlers who are both SKAN's partners and competitors, the fastest-growing buyer groups are the most price-sensitive, and SKAN's premium is visible in tenders.

Reasoning: In Porter terms (used here as a disqualification screen, as the Wisdom Library advises): supplier power low (in-house steel); buyer power medium and rising (concentrated top clients: in 2020 the top five took 43% of order intake; CDMO consolidation; dual sourcing); rivalry rising; entry barriers high for genuinely new firms but low for adjacent filler OEMs; substitutes weakening (RABS losing to isolators under Annex 1). No force disqualifies the industry; two (rivalry and buyer power) are moving the wrong way.

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?F4431st of 41full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

C+6013th of 41

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Substitutes: how much of a threat, and how is it changing?

Judgement: The threat from substitutes is modest and falling for new commercial lines, because Annex 1 favours sealed isolators over open cleanrooms and over restricted access barrier systems (RABS). It is not zero: cost-conscious mid-sized manufacturers upgrade RABS instead of buying isolators (Siegfried says isolators are not needed for every line), closed-vial and ready-to-use component systems simplify some processes, and in the long term oral peptides could reduce the injectable volume (GLP-1). None of these is material within 18 months.

Reasoning: Isolators are "the technology of choice"; over 90% of new lines integrate isolators (Optima expert). The substitution risk sits mainly in retrofits of existing lines, where RABS upgrades are cheaper.

Memo topic 3, : the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

D5623rd of 418 answers
Judgement56C-4160C-4245C-4364C-4463C-4545C-4640C-4756
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

D5623rd of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

SKAN's competitive advantage is migrating from the product to the installed base. Its new-equipment position remains leading in complex, toxic and high-speed lines, where it can price at a premium, but is eroding in standard lines as buyers move to turnkey suppliers. Its aftermarket position (mandatory requalification, validated consumables, software) is strong and widening, and guarantees S&C revenue growth, but has not so far protected S&C margin. For the model, the moat justifies confidence in revenue (backlog and S&C) but not in price or in a return of S&C margins to 2024 levels; any margin expansion must be earned through utilisation, PAS and cost discipline.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

D6027th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Sustainable competitive advantages: which, how strong, widening or narrowing?

Judgement: SKAN has two moats of different quality. The aftermarket moat (regulatory validation lock-in of the installed base, mandatory annual requalification, proprietary consumables and software written into validated processes) is strong and widening. The new-equipment moat (technology leadership and reference status in complex isolators) is real but narrowing in standard lines, because each new line is a fresh tender and filling-machine makers can now offer acceptable isolators bundled with their fillers. The investment case therefore rests increasingly on the aftermarket moat, which today produces about 35% to 39% of sales.

By type (naming the switching cost, as the Wisdom Library advises):

  • Customer-related, strong and widening. Procedural and regulatory switching cost: once a drug process is validated on a SKAN isolator, changing the isolator or its decontamination cycle requires revalidation and regulatory submissions over months to years. Risk switching cost: a contamination failure can cost a batch or a licence, so customers keep the original supplier for requalification (mandatory annually) and retrofits. Consumables: ABC Transfer bags and ports and Aseptic Technologies closed vials are written into validated processes (closed vials are used in eight approved drugs). Software: Metronik manufacturing execution systems and zenon integration raise integration costs of leaving. The installed base of about 1,100 to 1,150 isolators grows every year.
  • Production-related, medium and stable-to-narrowing. Proprietary electron-beam (about 50 units installed, "gold standard" for high-speed tub lines), H2O2 micro-nebulisation and nanox catalytic decontamination; aseptic-toxic containment expertise (DIN standards); in-house steelwork. Rivals are catching up (Syntegon's gloveless SynTiso, Getinge's ISOPRIME).
  • Scale-based, weak. SKAN is the largest isolator specialist but small against line builders (see C-42).
  • Regulatory and political, strong and stable. Reference status: nearly 700 regulators trained at Allschwil; co-authorship of ISPE containment guidance.
  • Culture and knowledge, medium. More than 50 years of isolator engineering, but rapid hiring (workforce roughly doubled in five years) dilutes it.

Why the distinction matters for the model: The aftermarket moat supports S&C revenue growth with high confidence (requalification is mandatory), but the evidence of the last eighteen months shows it has not protected S&C margins (29.5% to 20.1%), which means the moat protects revenue more than it protects price.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

F4532nd of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Scale relative to competitors and what it confers

Judgement: SKAN is large within isolators and small within its competitive arena. 2025 sales were CHF 333m with about 1,755 staff and an installed base of about 1,100 isolators; Syntegon reported EUR 1.75bn of sales and an installed base of about 72,000 machines (all types), Optima EUR 850m, Getinge about SEK 35bn group (Life Science SEK 4.6bn in 2024), Sartorius EUR 3.5bn. Within isolators, scale confers engineering depth, the broadest reference base, the ability to run parallel complex projects and to invest 7% to 8% of sales in R&D. It does not confer purchasing power, a cost advantage at line level, or the breadth of service network that a 72,000-machine base gives Syntegon.

Reasoning: Applying the Wisdom Library's "scale needs a bounded market" test: the isolator market is bounded, and SKAN's scale differential over other isolator specialists is large; but the boundary is porous, because filler makers can enter it from an adjacent market with their own scale. That makes SKAN's scale advantage partly temporary.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

D6322nd of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Surplus customer utility: is SKAN leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior?

Judgement: SKAN is demonstrably not inferior, and is leading, in complex lines (aseptic-toxic ADC lines, high-speed eBeam tub lines, cell and gene therapy) and in the service of its own installed base. In standard process-isolator lines it is no longer demonstrably superior, because the customer utility that is rising fastest is single-point accountability for the whole line, which SKAN cannot offer alone and turnkey rivals offer at lower prices.

Evidence that disproves inferiority in the complex end: near-universal blue-chip customer base (Novartis, Sandoz, GSK; Sanofi, Eli Lilly and Merck via consumables); recurring clients generated 71% of order intake over 2012 to 2023; a quotation success rate of about 50% despite a 30% to 50% price premium; customers prepay CHF 132m; it won most larger US fill-finish projects in H1 2026; regulators train on SKAN equipment; eBeam is the reference standard.

Evidence that inferiority is not disproven in standard lines: the competitor-reported fall in process-isolator share from about 70% to 50%; bids in which no one offers a third-party SKAN isolator; customers' stated preference for one purchase order; site-integration risk falling on SKAN's customers when the line is assembled for the first time at the site.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

D4526th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of surplus utility

Judgement: Demonstrated in services and spare parts, not demonstrated in equipment. In the aftermarket, SKAN raised prices on spare parts and consumables in 2025 to offset costs and tariffs, and blue-chip clients accepted them without negative feedback because maintenance is mission-critical. In equipment, the evidence runs the other way: an allocation market has become a tender market, the premium is visible, and fixed-price contracts carry inflation without indexation. The collapse in E&S margin in 2025 was mainly volume under-absorption, not price, but no evidence shows SKAN raising real equipment prices.

Reasoning: In Towson's taxonomy (Wisdom Library, "mission-critical products"), the isolator is a "critical" purchase for the customer (avoiding failure matters more than price), which should support pricing; the countervailing force is that a critical purchase from a turnkey supplier who also takes integration risk is even more attractive to the buyer. SKAN does not disclose price and volume effects, which is itself a gap.

Modelling implication: S&C price +2% to +4% a year; E&S price flat in real terms; no price recovery for cost inflation on existing backlog.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

F4033rd of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Does SKAN share scale economies with customers?

Judgement: Only incidentally. SKAN's strategy and messaging are about premium value capture (technology, quality, process warranty), not about passing scale savings to customers. Some benefits reach customers de facto: standardisation (VARIOSYS, modular components) shortens delivery and validation; VR mock-ups replace physical wooden mock-ups; PAS shortens customers' time to market; digital twins raise customers' equipment effectiveness. These are productivity gains shared as utility, not as lower prices.

Reasoning: A scale-economies-shared model would be a strong defence against turnkey rivals in standard lines; SKAN's premium pricing in a tender market points the other way. The new chief executive's choice between holding price and sharing standardisation savings to defend share is one of the unstated strategic decisions of the next two years.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

D5621st of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Do gravitational, mechanical forces improve SKAN's advantage and financials with each period?

Judgement: Yes in the aftermarket, no in new equipment. Mechanically, each isolator delivered adds 15 to 25 years of mandatory requalification, retrofits and spare parts; each drug approved on a closed vial or validated transfer bag adds a consumables stream for the life of the drug; each line commissioned adds to SKAN's reference base with regulators. These forces raise S&C revenue every year without heroic effort (installed base 1,000 to 1,150 and rising; service backlog CHF 79.8m). Against this, a mechanical force also works for competitors: every turnkey line a filler OEM delivers with its own isolator builds that OEM's validation history and weakens SKAN's specification position in the next bid.

Modelling implication: S&C organic revenue growth of 10% to 15% a year is supported by installed-base mechanics (organic S&C +14.8% in H1 2026); margin improvement is not mechanical and must come from management action.

Management is ambitious, candid and technically excellent, and has invested heavily ahead of demand; it has been less good at forecasting revenue timing, delivering regulatory milestones on time and protecting the services margin. C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

B689th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading Size of the company's ambition or vision, relative to peers

Judgement: The ambition is large and the largest among the peers in the files: triple sales (toward about CHF 1bn) and quadruple EBITDA in the mid term, mid-to-upper teens growth, a 50/50 recurring mix, a new pay-per-use service business, global expansion and digitalisation. Sartorius targets 8% to 11% growth, Getinge 3% to 6% organic, Tecan more than 4% with a cost-led margin plan. SKAN's ambition is growth-first and backed by real investment. The weakness is that ambition has repeatedly run ahead of delivery (2024 and 2025 revenue misses), so the market discounts it.

Reasoning: The ambition was set by the previous chief executive and presented by the CFO. The new chief executive has reaffirmed it but emphasises operational excellence, which suggests the ambition will be pursued with more discipline, and perhaps recalibrated in time.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

C-6019th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading Quality of the strategy and the boldness and rightness of priorities

Judgement: The priorities are right for widening the moat (installed-base services, validated consumables, software, standardisation, US presence, complex high-value lines) and include one bold move (PAS). The strategy is weaker on the most important competitive threat, the rebundling of the fill-finish line: SKAN's answer is partnership with filler makers rather than an integrated offer of its own, which leaves its specification position dependent on partners who also compete with it. Dynamism is moderate: pre-producing standard isolators during 2025's gaps was a good adaptive move, but the cost base was built for growth and cannot flex quickly in a downturn.

Reasoning: A strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments needs either a flexible cost base or a recurring revenue base large enough to absorb equipment downturns. SKAN is building the second (S&C 39%) but is not there yet.

C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

D5529th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great is the chief executive, judged by actions?

Judgement: Promising but unproven: one half-year in office, no completed guidance cycle, and a pedigree that is strong but not yet translated into visible SKAN-specific decisions beyond a new segment-accountability structure. His actions so far are consistent with a disciplined operator: confirming, not raising, guidance despite strong orders; openly disclosing the CHF 15m to 20m cancellation exposure and excluding it from planning; keeping operating expense growth (13.8%) well below sales growth (24.0% at constant currency); prioritising US fill-finish wins and next-generation eBeam.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • Background: five years as head of Mettler-Toledo's Product Inspection Division and member of its Group Executive Board, a business with pharmaceutical quality-control exposure and a culture known for pricing and productivity discipline; he inherited the CHF 442.3m backlog and the 2026 plan from his predecessor.
  • The case against: his independent capital-allocation record is unverifiable (Mettler's matrix kept final financial authority at group level); the early recovery may reflect late-cycle tailwinds; he starts with a CFO under conviction.
  • What would prove him: an H2 2026 delivered without slippage; a 2027 guide set at a beatable level; a dated PAS approval; a visible narrowing of the H1/H2 margin gap; a decision on the CFO succession if needed.

F4437th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading Priority on hiring well, and the quality of the leadership team

Judgement: The team is experienced but thin and currently weakened, and the chief executive has not yet visibly built his own team. Strengths: long-tenured technical leaders (CTO Ralf Krämer and Chief Contracting Officer Thomas Zinn since 2020, Martin Steegmüller since 2017), structured internal promotion, an HR leader in place since 2022, and a board addition with software expertise. Weaknesses: the CFO is convicted at first instance and on appeal; the Chief Service Officer resigned and services leadership is doubled up on one executive just as services became the strategic priority; succession planning below the chief executive is informal; hiring specialists in a tight Swiss market is hard.

Reasoning: Under normal governance a conviction would trigger an exit; the board's choice keeps the experienced CFO but extends uncertainty. If the appeal fails, the new chief executive will hire a CFO, which could reset financial discipline, but the transition would coincide with his first year.

C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Very well resourced, arguably over-resourced relative to current demand. The buffer side is also adequate: CHF 88.5m cash, low net debt, customer prepayments.

C-61

How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

F3641st of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading Record of acting and delivering, beyond guidance

Judgement: Mixed to weak over the last three years, strong over the longer pre-2024 period. The company grew sales at a 17% to 19% CAGR from 2019 to 2024 and delivered its 2023 targets; then it missed revenue guidance in 2024 and badly in 2025, slipped the PAS timeline three times, and underperformed peers in the 2025 environment (Syntegon grew EBITDA 27% in 2025 while SKAN's fell 32%). It has delivered on actions it controls (acquisitions closed and consolidated, capacity built, standard-isolator pre-production) and less well on outcomes that depend on customers and regulators.

Reasoning: The pattern is of a company that does what it says on investment and organisation, and over-promises on timing of revenue and approvals. That pattern should lean the second set's forecasts toward later timing, not lower end-states.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

F3834th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading Do they put shareholders first, and how prudent is governance? What does the evidence show, and why?

Judgement: Governance is prudent in its controls and modest in pay, but not shareholder-first in its structure or in its most recent decision. The controls are sound: a single share class with one vote per share, no related-party transactions, board approval for any investment of CHF 1m or more, internal audit by KPMG, clean audit opinions from BDO with no key audit matters in 2025, a whistleblowing system, modest pay (former CEO CHF 0.86m in 2025, no bonus) and no severance. The structure and decisions favour insiders and stability: about a third of the shares sit with two family groups; registration limits and two-thirds supermajorities entrench the status quo; the audit committee includes a 10.6% shareholder; the former CEO sits on the board; executives had no long-term equity incentive until 2026; and the board retained a CFO convicted of insider trading at first instance.

Why: SKAN is a recently listed (2021), family-anchored Swiss company. Its governance prioritises continuity, loyalty to long-serving people and protection from outside pressure. That is defensible on the presumption of innocence, but it is not the scaffolding a shareholder-first board builds in advance; the 2023 proxy vote against four directors shows institutional investors have noticed.

C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

F4030th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading Is SKAN an enlightened capital allocator and dynamic repurchaser?

Judgement: No, not yet. SKAN is an operator and investor, not a capital allocator in the Lifco or Mettler sense. Allocation has been growth-first: capacity and people ahead of demand, a large bet on PAS, small debt-funded acquisitions at undisclosed prices (my estimate about three times sales), a 30% payout dividend, and no buybacks (treasury purchases only for incentive plans). The five-year free cash flow is negative, and the return on capital deployed since the IPO is not yet demonstrated. 4c

Financial profile

F4833rd of 418 answers
Judgement48C-4950C-5055C-5152C-5232C-5344C-5460C-7672
Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

F4833rd of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

SKAN's economics are those of a high-value engineering business: high gross value-add, mid-teens EBITDA margins at best so far, steep downside operating leverage, low financial leverage, and poor cash conversion caused by heavy pre-investment and by working capital that swings with order intake. EPS is a geared derivative of project timing; the cost base has stepped up so far that 2027 consensus EPS is barely above 2024's. The upside lies in three concentrated levers (PAS, E&S utilisation, S&C margin) that are congruent with strategy and could lift margins to the upper teens, but none is yet proven and consensus already assumes most of the path. For the model: build costs as a fixed block plus variable materials, model by half-year, link advances to orders, put PAS on a separate ramp, and keep a 2025-type downside scenario.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

D5023rd of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading EPS growth, revenue growth, and durability

Judgement: SKAN's EPS is a highly geared derivative of project timing rather than a steady compounder. EPS rose from CHF 0.84 (2021) to 1.73 (2024), a 27% CAGR on revenue growth of 15% a year, then fell 58% to 0.72 in 2025 on a 7.7% revenue decline. Consensus expects 1.40 (+94%), 1.84 (+32%), 2.39 (+30%) and 3.08 (+29%) for 2026 to 2029 on revenue growth of 19%, 15%, 16% and 20%. The key insight for the model is that 2027 consensus EPS (1.84) is only 6% above 2024's 1.73 despite revenue 26% higher (454 against 361): the cost base stepped up by roughly CHF 15m to 20m since 2024 (headcount, acquisitions, PAS pre-launch costs), so the business needs about CHF 90m more revenue to regain its 2024 margin.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • EPS growth roughly twice revenue growth in up-years (operating leverage, low tax of 13% to 18%, little interest), and roughly eight times revenue decline in down-years.
  • Consensus EBITDA margin 2027 15.4% against 15.8% in 2024: no net margin progress in three years.
  • Durability: low over any single year (one delayed customer can move EPS by 20% or more); moderate over three years (backlog, regulation). EPS dilution risks: minorities about 10% of net income, rising depreciation as PAS assets go live, interest on CHF 131m of debt.
  • Precedent within the files: Tecan (EPS from CHF 12.88 to 6.87 in two years) shows how far EPS can fall when a tools company's cycle and cost base go wrong together.

Modelling implication: Build EPS from segment EBITDA, not from a margin assumption on group sales; include D&A rising from about 13 to 16 to 18 as PAS depreciates, minorities at about 10%, tax at 15% to 18%.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

D5521st of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading Scope to deploy capital at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years

Judgement: The scope is large but the evidence of high returns on the capital already deployed is weak, so the fair multiple should depend on proof from PAS and capacity utilisation in 2027 to 2028. Between 2021 and 2025 SKAN invested about CHF 206m in capex and about CHF 85m to 90m in acquisitions; EBITDA went from 32.4 (2021) to 57.0 (2024) and back to 38.6 (2025). The return on that capital is back-ended: PAS (about CHF 70m) earns nothing yet; Görlitz, Stein and office capacity is under-utilised in H1 periods.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • PAS, if it reaches about CHF 50m of revenue at 40% to 50% EBITDA margin, would earn CHF 20m to 25m of EBITDA on about CHF 70m of capital: a pre-tax return above 25%. That is the single largest high-return deployment available. Management says ramp-up takes about three years from commercial start, with meaningful revenue only from 2027.
  • Growth capex of CHF 100m to 120m over the mid term was guided at the CMD; consensus capex of 30 to 38 a year implies most of it is spent.
  • Further opportunities: consumables capacity (Aseptic Technologies, ABC Transfer, Plast4Life moulding), US service and assembly, digital. These are smaller, with returns tied to installed-base attach.
  • Customer advances fund working capital, so incremental growth needs little working capital, which raises returns on growth capital if advances hold.

Implication for the fair multiple: If PAS and utilisation deliver, SKAN combines mid-teens growth with high incremental returns and deserves a premium multiple (its ten-year median 24-month-forward P/E of 32.7x). If they do not, it is a mid-teens-margin capital-goods company with lumpy earnings, for which its tenth-percentile 23.9x is the more appropriate anchor.

C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

D5221st of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading Returns on capital

Judgement: Returns are moderate and flattered by accounting and by customer advances. Company-reported return on capital employed was 15.8% (2021), 15.3% (2022), 19.7% (2023), 21.5% (2024) and 10.3% (2025). These are pre-tax and exclude goodwill, because under Swiss GAAP FER SKAN offsets acquired goodwill against equity at acquisition, and they benefit from negative net working capital funded by customer prepayments. On a post-tax basis including acquired goodwill, my rough estimate for 2026 (EBIT about 41, tax about 17%, capital employed about CHF 170m excluding and about CHF 260m including offset goodwill) is about 20% excluding and about 13% including goodwill.

Reasoning: Post-tax ROCE including goodwill in the low teens is comfortably above a cost of capital of about 7% to 9%, but well below the peers often used for comparison (GEA's ROIC about 38%; Sartorius targets 9.5% ROIC by 2029 because of acquisitions; Getinge 11.6%). The return on tangible capital is high because the business is engineering labour, not plant: maintenance capex is only about 3% of sales.

Modelling implication: Do not use reported ROCE for valuation without adding back offset goodwill; track the return on the PAS asset base separately.

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

F3237th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading Conversion of net income into free cash flow, and the behaviour of working capital

Judgement: Cash conversion has been poor and is the weakest element of the economic profile. On FactSet's definition (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex), free cash flow was -39.3, +30.0, -30.6, -12.2 and +12.0 in 2021 to 2025: cumulative -40m against cumulative net income of about 130m. Consensus expects only 8, 18 and 22 for 2026 to 2028 (conversion of 26% to 44% of net income, against peers at 1.0x to 1.4x). Working capital is structurally negative because customers prepay (advances CHF 132m at mid-2026, about 36% of trailing sales), so operating cash flow swings with order intake, not with profit.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • 2023 shows the downside: order intake fell 28% and operating cash flow fell to CHF 8.7m from 61.5m. 2025 shows the upside: advances rose by about CHF 39m and operating cash flow reached 64.1m despite EBITDA falling to 38.6. H1 2026 shows the in-between: EBITDA up to 15.3 but operating cash flow down 27.7% to 16.3; company free cash flow 2.7.
  • Company net working capital: -26.6 (end-2025), -30.5 (mid-2026), versus +8.2 (end-2024). FactSet's receivables plus inventory less payables is a positive 118 to 128 because it excludes advances and work in progress; use the company definition.
  • Prepayments and accrued income rose from 11.8 to 19.0 in H1 2026 and receivables fell; work in progress 82.0.

Modelling implication: Model advances as a function of order intake and backlog (roughly 30% of backlog), capex falling from 13% to 7% to 8% of sales, and expect conversion to improve toward 50% to 70% of net income by 2028 only if order intake keeps growing; in any year with falling orders, free cash flow can turn negative even with stable profit.

C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

F4437th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading Gross and operating margins and their trend

Judgement: Group margins are mid-teens at best and below peers, with a downward trend in the segment that is supposed to lift them. Group EBITDA margin: 13.7% (2021), 14.5% (2022), 15.7% (2023), 15.8% (2024), 11.6% (2025), 9.3% (H1 2026); consensus 14.1% (2026), 15.4% (2027), 16.6% (2028), 17.3% (2029). E&S: 11.2% (2024), 4.3% (2025), 2.3% (H1 2026), but 14% to 15% in recent second halves. S&C: 28.1% (2023), 29.5% (2024), 25.0% (2025), 20.1% (H1 2026). Gross margin (sales less materials, 70% to 79% depending on definition) is high and stable because SKAN's value-add is engineering labour; it says little about profitability.

Reasoning and the margin decomposition the model needs:

  • The mix-shift thesis assumes S&C carries 25% to 30%. At a 50/50 mix, S&C at 28% and E&S at 12% give a 20% group margin; S&C at 20% and E&S at 12% give 16%.
  • Consensus 2028 (sales 525, EBITDA 87.2, 16.6%) is consistent with, for example, E&S sales about 300 at about 12% and S&C (including PAS) about 225 at about 22%. Consensus therefore does not require S&C to return to 29.5%. It requires E&S at about 12% for a full year (above 2024's 11.2%, on about 10% more E&S revenue than 2024) and S&C at about 22% including PAS, which is roughly today's S&C margin plus a PAS contribution of the order of CHF 20m of revenue at about 40% in 2028.
  • Peer margins (brokers' consensus 2026 to 2028): Getinge 21%, Sartorius 30% to 31%, GEA 17% to 18%, Tecan 17% to 19%; Syntegon 16.1% in 2025. SKAN's 14% to 17% is the lowest.
  • Seasonality: H1 margins are structurally lower (13.1% to 13.3% in normal years, 0.7% in 2025, 9.3% in 2026) than H2 (17.5% to 18.9%), because project milestones cluster in H2. Model by half-year.

Modelling implication: Use E&S full-year margin of 9% to 13% for 2026 to 2028, S&C 20% to 24% before PAS, and add PAS separately at a ramping margin; group margin of 14% to 17% follows.

Continued in ANSWERS-part5.md

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

B+606th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How clean is the earnings base?

Judgement: Reasonably clean in the narrow sense (adjusted and reported earnings are almost identical: brokers' 2025 EPS 0.720 against reported 0.724; R&D of 7% to 8% of sales is fully expensed), but not comparable with IFRS peers and with two judgement-heavy areas to watch. (1) Swiss GAAP FER lets SKAN offset acquired goodwill against equity, so no goodwill amortisation or impairment ever passes through the income statement; IFRS peers show amortisation of acquired intangibles (and then usually add it back in "adjusted" figures). (2) Revenue on custom projects is recognised by percentage of completion measured by cost incurred against planned cost, which makes the H1/H2 split and the timing of profit sensitive to estimates and to customer delays. Tax and minorities are modest but real leakages.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • Goodwill offset: the 2025 acquisitions (about CHF 85m to 90m) cut the reported equity ratio to 26.1% (adjusted above 40%). If SKAN amortised that goodwill over 5 to 20 years, EBIT would be about CHF 4m to 18m a year lower; the files do not give the figure (Swiss GAAP FER requires a shadow disclosure in the notes; worth retrieving).
  • Inventory allowances: the allowance for obsolete and slow-moving items rose from CHF 3.3m to 4.4m in 2025 while inventory fell from 36.2 to 33.1; a project-related allowance of CHF 16.1m was released in 2023 (total allowances fell from 18.6 to 2.7). The files do not say whether that release passed through the 2023 income statement; if it did, it was a third of 2023 EBITDA of CHF 50.1m. This is the single most important earnings-quality question in the history and should be checked before using 2023 as a base year.
  • Capitalised development: the Risks report notes capitalised R&D "hours" rising from 475.1 to 766.5; the Earnings Review says SKAN expenses all R&D. Either way the amount is immaterial.
  • Tax: 12.8% to 17.8% in 2021 to 2025; the OECD minimum tax and Swiss cantonal changes point to the upper end (15% to 18%).
  • Minorities: about 11% of H1 2026 net profit (5.4 total, 4.8 attributable); 11.8m in the balance sheet.
  • Currency: foreign-exchange losses on US dollar assets hit the financial result in 2025; lower FX volatility improved it in H1 2026.

Modelling implication: Use reported figures, model tax at 16% to 18% and minorities at about 10% of net income, and flag that SKAN's P/E is not comparable with IFRS peers' "adjusted" P/E only in that both exclude acquisition amortisation; SKAN's exclusion is permanent and invisible.

C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

B728th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading Financial leverage and gearing

Judgement: Financial leverage is low and comfortable: net debt CHF 42.2m at mid-2026 (debt 131, cash 88.5), 0.8x trailing EBITDA of about CHF 53m, fixed-rate bank debt, net interest of about CHF 1.5m in 2025. It barely amplifies EPS. The hidden leverage is operational: customer advances of CHF 132m are a working-capital liability that would reverse in a downturn. A 30% fall in advances (about CHF 40m) would roughly double net debt to about CHF 80m, or about 1.5x.

Reasoning: Unused credit lines fell from CHF 66m to 32m after the acquisitions, so liquidity headroom is smaller than a year ago, but there is no refinancing or covenant concern in the files. The reported equity ratio of 26.1% understates solvency because of the goodwill offset.

Modelling implication: Interest expense CHF 2m to 3m a year; net debt flat to slowly falling; treat advances as cyclical.

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)
