5a

Pricing power

3 answers
C-1746C-4545P-0446
C-17To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?D4624th of 41full answer under 2b Internal corporate change forces
C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?D4526th of 41full answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

D4615th of 27

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Price will add about 1% to 1.5% a year to group revenue and roughly nothing net to the group margin, because it is concentrated in services and consumables, where it mainly offsets wage inflation, and absent in equipment, where tenders and turnkey bundling hold real prices flat to slightly down. Price is therefore not a margin lever in my model: margin expansion comes from volume absorbing the fixed base (P-01) and from PAS, not from price. The risk is asymmetric: a strong franc and the turnkey shift could make equipment prices fall in real terms in 2028 to 2029 if the pharma capital-spending plateau leaves suppliers chasing fewer projects, while the upside (a Mettler-style value-pricing programme under the new chief executive) is unannounced.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • Services, spare parts and consumables (S&C, about 38% to 44% of revenue in my forecast): SKAN raised spare-part and consumable prices in 2025 to offset costs and tariffs, and blue-chip customers accepted them without significant pushback (Q2 2025 call, 19-Aug-25); requalification is mandatory, and closed vials and transfer bags are written into validated processes. I model +2% to +4% a year on the legacy S&C base (about CHF 130m to 220m), adding CHF 3m to 6m of revenue a year at a high drop-through. But the AlphaSense pricing report notes that the 7% to 15% increases quoted for the aftermarket are the same figures as reported for new equipment contracts in 2022, so realised aftermarket price is not independently measured, and the S&C margin fell from 29.5% to 20.1% while prices were rising: price has not protected margin, because the segment's cost base grew faster.
  • Equipment (E&S, about 56% to 62% of revenue): no evidence of like-for-like real price increases since 2023. The market moved from allocation to tenders; the quotation success rate is about 50%; the historical premium of up to 30%, sometimes 50%, over competitors is described by an Optima manager as nominal against premier European systems and material only against standard tiers; price-conscious contract manufacturers and biosimilar makers force comparable pricing; filling-machine makers discount their own isolators when bundled. Rising average project values (+15% to 20% a year on complexity) are scope, not price. I model zero real price in E&S, which in nominal terms means about 1% to 2% a year, matching cost inflation with a lag. Since 2022 new contracts carry inflation clauses (a correction to the first set; see the orientation), so cost inflation on backlog is partly passed on.
  • The franc: because most capital projects are priced in francs, a stronger franc makes SKAN dearer for euro and dollar buyers without hurting the margin on contracts already signed; the effect arrives as pricing pressure on new tenders against Syntegon, Optima and Groninger, who price in euros. Morgan Stanley expects EUR/CHF at 0.89 and USD/CHF at 0.82 in 2027; ING about 0.90 for EUR/CHF. In my base case this costs about 1 to 1.5 points of reported growth a year (translation of foreign service revenue and price concessions), which is inside my revenue forecasts.
  • Tariffs: US-bound isolators ship from Görlitz under EU rates (about 15%) on Delivered-at-Place terms, so the duty is the customer's; spare-part price rises offset tariffs on parts imported by SKAN US. Neutral for the margin in the base case; a risk if customers increasingly ask SKAN to absorb duty.
  • PAS pricing is untested: no contracts have been disclosed; management cites strong interest because pre-qualified lines shorten time to market. I assume the PAS margin reaches about 40% at 25 to 30 of revenue (not the "up to 50%" at 50 of revenue), which implicitly assumes no premium pricing beyond management's plan.
  • Peer reference: Mettler-Toledo raised its pricing contribution from 200 to 350 basis points (Morgan Stanley, 1-Dec-25); large equipment companies achieved catch-up pricing of 2% to 3% after the pandemic (Bernstein); Getinge offset a SEK 1bn tariff and currency headwind partly by price. SKAN's equipment pricing sits below all of them because it sells a component into a line that others increasingly sell as a package.

Line-item implications: price contributes about 1 to 1.5 points of the 9% to 13% annual revenue growth (S&C about 3 points of its growth, E&S about 1 point nominal); price is margin-neutral in the base case; a 2% real equipment price decline in 2028 to 2029 (the downside) would cost about CHF 6m of EBITDA a year, about 1.2 points of margin.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7544P-0148
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

F4438th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading Fixed-cost operating leverage: how much, and how wide a range of outcomes?

Judgement: Operating leverage is high and asymmetric. On the way down SKAN loses 65% to 70% of lost revenue at EBITDA (FY 2025 66%; H1 2025 71%), because personnel (about half of sales) and other operating costs are fixed over a twelve-month horizon and only materials (about a quarter of sales) flex. On the way up, management has chosen to reinvest, so incremental margins were only 17% to 23% in 2023 and 2024 and 48% in H1 2026; consensus implies about 24% for 2026 to 2028. The range of EPS outcomes around any year's consensus is therefore wide and skewed to the downside.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • Within E&S, half-year evidence implies a contribution margin of about 75% to 80% on volume and a fixed cost base of roughly CHF 75m to 80m per half-year (my estimate from H1 2025 to H2 2025: sales +35.4, EBITDA +27.6). At full-year level the E&S drop-through was about 40% (2024 to 2025: sales -54, EBITDA -21), because fixed costs also moved.
  • Management's own sensitivities (Change Forces report): a 2 percentage point change in project delivery volumes changes EPS by about 12.2%; 1 point of revenue growth changes EPS by about 7.9%; 0.5 points of EBIT margin changes EPS by about 5%.
  • My illustration on 2027 consensus (sales 454, EBITDA 70.1, EPS 1.84): a 5% revenue shortfall at a 50% decremental cuts EPS to about 1.42; a 10% shortfall at 60% (a 2025-type year) cuts it to about 0.85; a 5% beat at the usual 25% incremental lifts it to about 2.05 (holding the consensus ratio of net income to pre-tax profit constant). The 2025 precedent (sales -7.7%, EPS -58%) shows the downside case is not theoretical.

Modelling implication: Model costs as a fixed block (personnel by headcount and wage inflation, other opex) plus variable materials at 23% to 26% of sales; apply a 60% to 70% decremental in downside scenarios and a 20% to 30% incremental in base-case growth years unless management signals a hiring pause.

P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

C-4812th of 27

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Operating leverage will lift the EBITDA margin from 11.6% (2025) to 14.1% (2026), 15.5% (2027), 16.5% (2028) and about 17.3% by 2030, but this is a recovery of lost margin on a cost base that stepped up during 2023 to 2025, not a new level of profitability: the EBITDA margin passes its 2024 peak (15.8%) only in 2028, and the EBIT margin passes its 2024 peak (12.3%) only in 2028, on about 34% more revenue. The leverage is real and large in both directions, but management has historically chosen to reinvest most of the upside (incremental margins of 17% to 23% in 2023 and 2024) while bearing almost all of the downside (decremental margins of 66% to 71% in 2025). I model a 26% to 27% incremental EBITDA margin in 2027 and 2028, at the top of SKAN's growth-year history and in line with consensus, and treat a higher incremental margin as the bull case and a 2025-type decremental as the bear case. The single most important point for the third set: the cost structure turns a revenue forecast error into an EPS error roughly four to five times larger.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • The cost structure (2025, company-reported): personnel CHF 167.4m (50.2% of sales; 55.3% in H1 2026), material and external services 24% to 27% of sales (24.3% in H1 2026), other operating expenses CHF 50.6m (15.2%), partly offset by own work capitalised and other income. Only materials flex with volume within a year; personnel and other expenses behave as fixed over twelve months, because aseptic engineers take about a year to train and are needed when delayed projects resume. On a contribution basis about 75% of each franc of revenue is available to cover a fixed block of about CHF 230m to 240m (my derivation for 2026: revenue 387 less materials at 24.5% less EBITDA 54.5).
  • Why the measured incremental margin is so much lower than the 75% contribution margin: the fixed block itself grows every year. Wage inflation of 2% to 3% on about CHF 240m adds CHF 5m to 7m; every 1% of extra headcount adds about CHF 2m; PAS operators, quality staff and US service staff are being added before revenue. In H1 2026 operating expenses rose 13.8% against constant-currency sales growth of 24.0%, and, after stripping out about seven months of acquired cost base (Metronik and ABC Transfer, consolidated from August 2025), organic cost growth appears to have been about 6% to 8% (my estimate). If that discipline held through 2027 the incremental margin would be about 40% and 2027 EBITDA about 76 (17.4%). I do not put that in the base case because SKAN's revealed behaviour in two growth years was to reinvest, because PAS start-up costs come before revenue, and because the S&C margin decline (29.5% to 20.1%) is not yet explained or reversed. It is the clearest source of upside to my margins.
  • Half-year phasing dominates the reported leverage. Under cost-to-cost percentage of completion, design is 5% to 10% of project value and commissioning 10% to 15%, while manufacturing and assembly carry 75% to 80%. H1 margins in normal years were 13.1% to 13.3% and H2 margins 17.5% to 18.9%; H1 2025 was 0.7% and H1 2026 9.3%. I model H2 2026 at 17.6%, H1 2027 at 12.0% and H2 2027 at 18.1%: the H1/H2 gap narrows only slowly. A narrowing of that gap in H1 2027 (to 13% or more) would be the first hard evidence that the new chief executive has changed execution.
  • The leverage by segment. E&S: within one half-year the drop-through was about 78% (H1 to H2 2025: sales +35.4, EBITDA +27.6), but year on year about 40% in both directions (2024 to 2025: sales -54, EBITDA -21). I model E&S margins of 9.7% (2026), 11.0% (2027), 11.8% (2028) and about 11.6% to 11.8% thereafter, deliberately capped near the 2024 level (11.2% on 271 of sales) because tender pricing and turnkey competition absorb part of the volume leverage (see P-04). S&C: the fixed base was built ahead (service staff, PAS pre-operating costs, acquisitions), so S&C carries the more reliable leverage from 2027, from 22.0% (2026) to 22.6%, 23.4% and about 24.3%; it does not return to the 28% to 29.5% of 2023 to 2024, because the acquired businesses carry lower margins and PAS adds a fixed cost block.
  • Against the last four to six years. From 2020 to 2024 the margin rose by about half a point a year (13.7%, 14.5%, 15.7%, 15.8%) while revenue grew 17% a year, because management hired ahead (216 staff in 2023, 284 in 2025 including 208 by acquisition) and raised R&D and strategic spending to 8.3% of sales (2024). From 2026 to 2030 I expect slower revenue growth (9% to 13%) but faster margin progress (about one point a year), because the capacity is already built: the step-up in fixed cost happened in 2023 to 2025 and the coming years absorb it. Personnel as a share of sales should fall from 50.2% (2025) to about 47% to 48% (2026) and about 44% to 45% by 2028 to 2030. The price of the earlier build-out is visible in the comparison with 2024: 2027 revenue of 436 is 21% above 2024, yet 2027 EBIT (50.1) is only 13% above 2024's 44.5.
  • The downside arithmetic. On my 2027 base, a revenue shortfall of CHF 10m at a 60% to 70% decremental costs CHF 6m to 7m of EBITDA and about CHF 0.23 of EPS (14% of EPS). A 2025-type year (revenue 7% below base, 60% decremental) takes 2027 EPS to about 1.00. Management's own sensitivity (Change Forces report) is that one point of revenue growth moves EPS by about 7.9%; my figures give about 5% (one point is CHF 3.9m of revenue, at a 65% decremental about CHF 2.5m of EBITDA and CHF 0.09 of EPS), and management's figure implies near-full drop-through. Either way, a revenue error becomes an EPS error four to eight times larger.

Peer read-across: Syntegon (EBITDA +27% on record 2025 sales of EUR 1.75bn, 16.1% margin) shows the upside when a fill-finish equipment maker's volume flows and it has a large service base; Stevanato (Engineering segment -12% in Q3 2026, start-up costs at Fishers and Latina) and Tecan (5% workforce cut in H2 2024, then "Rewired" savings of CHF 18m in H1 2026) show the downside and how slowly self-help offsets it. SKAN's profile sits between the two: more volume leverage than Tecan, less service ballast than Syntegon.

Line-item implications (in the forecast inputs): EBITDA margin 14.1%, 15.5%, 16.5%, 17.0%, 17.3% (2026 to 2030); EBIT margin 10.6%, 11.5%, 12.5%, 13.0%, 13.4%; personnel ratio falling about one point a year; materials 24% to 25%; no restructuring charges. The margin path matches consensus (14.1%, 15.4%, 16.6%, 17.3%); my difference to consensus is the revenue on which it is earned.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7672P-0260
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?B728th of 41full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

C+606th of 27

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Financial leverage is low, cheap and falling, and it will barely affect EPS: net interest of CHF 1.5m to 2.5m a year is 3% to 6% of pre-tax profit, and net debt falls from CHF 42.2m (mid-2026, 0.8x EBITDA) to about 29m at end-2026, 19m at end-2027 and about 6m at end-2028, before a probable CHF 25m outflow for Metronik's minority stake from 2029. The real leverage on SKAN's balance sheet is not its bank debt but its customer advances (CHF 132.2m at mid-2026, about 36% of trailing sales), which fund the working capital and turn into a cash outflow if orders fall. For EPS the balance sheet is neutral to mildly positive; for cash and for downside risk the advances are the variable that matters.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • The debt: CHF 120m drawn on an unsecured syndicated facility (Zürcher Kantonalbank, Commerzbank, BNP Paribas) at 0.95% (SARON plus a margin), due within one to five years, plus euro bank loans of CHF 7.1m at 1.25% to 3.00%; FactSet total debt 131 includes about CHF 4m of leases. Cash of CHF 88.5m earned almost nothing (CHF 2 thousand of third-party interest in 2025). Covenants are not disclosed. Because the reported equity ratio is low (26% to 27%) owing to the goodwill offset (44% adjusted), an equity-ratio covenant, if one exists, would be the binding constraint; this is worth asking about but does not affect the base case.
  • Rates: markets price two Swiss National Bank hikes by September 2027 (ING). A 100 basis point rise in SARON on about CHF 120m costs CHF 1.2m, about 3% of 2027 net income. The fragility is small.
  • Net financial items include more than interest: foreign-exchange results on dollar assets (losses in 2025), the unwinding of the discount on the ABC Transfer liability, and facility fees. 2025 pre-tax profit (21.4) was CHF 4.0m below EBIT (25.4); H1 2026 implies about CHF 2m. I model -3.5 (2026), -2.5 (2027), -2.0 (2028 and 2029) and -1.5 (2030). Consensus implies -2.4, -1.4 and -0.9: about CHF 0.04 a share more optimistic than me in 2027 and 2028, a small but real part of my gap to consensus.
  • The advances loop: customers typically pay about 30% on order; advances rose from 84.7 (end-2024) to 123.8 (end-2025) and 132.2 (mid-2026). In 2023, when orders fell 28%, operating cash flow fell to 8.7 from 61.5. A 30% fall in advances (about CHF 40m) would roughly double net debt to about CHF 80m (about 1.5x my 2026 EBITDA). This is the balance-sheet transmission of the 2028 capex-plateau risk (see P-05): it would hit free cash flow a year before it hits EBITDA.
  • Deferred acquisition liabilities: the remaining 16% of ABC Transfer (a committed purchase; I assume about CHF 3m in 2027, timing not disclosed) and the put and call on Metronik's 24% from 2029 at the earliest, at fair market value at exercise. Metronik's goodwill of CHF 75.5m on a 76% stake implies a 100% value of roughly CHF 100m to 110m at acquisition, so the 24% would cost roughly CHF 25m if exercised in 2029, more if Metronik grows (my estimate). I place it in 2029 and remove most minority leakage from 2030.
  • What financial leverage does for EPS: nothing much in either direction. There are no buybacks (treasury purchases only to cover incentive plans, which I model at about CHF 1m a year), the dividend follows a 30% payout policy, and leverage headroom (unused credit lines fell from CHF 66m to 32m) rules out large debt-funded deals without equity. By end-2028 the balance sheet is close to net cash, which, with free cash flow of 25 to 50 a year from 2027, creates the first real capacity for capital returns or bolt-on M&A in 2029 to 2030. That optionality is not in my numbers.

Line-item implications: net financial items -3.5 to -1.5; net debt to EBITDA 0.5x (2026), 0.3x (2027), about 0.1x (2028 and 2029), net cash in 2030; acquisitions -3 (2027) and -25 (2029); dividend per share 0.40, 0.51, 0.62, 0.71, 0.83 (30% of my EPS, declared for the year and paid the following spring).

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7752P-0352
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

D5225th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How much of each major cost block is cuttable?

Judgement: In a downturn SKAN could reduce perhaps 10% to 15% of its non-material cost base (roughly CHF 20m to 35m) within a year without destroying capacity, mainly from discretionary R&D and strategic-initiative spending, temporary staff and other operating expenses. The core engineering workforce is effectively not cuttable without damaging the franchise, because specialists are scarce, take years to train and are needed when delayed projects resume. Management's revealed preference in 2025 was not to cut at all (no restructuring, pre-production of standard isolators instead).

By block (2025 approximate): personnel CHF 167m (50% of sales): largely fixed; temporary staff (about 139 temporary employees in late 2023) and overtime are the flexible part; Swiss specialists are hard to replace. R&D and strategic initiatives (7% to 8% of sales, about CHF 25m to 30m, partly within personnel): partly deferrable; management said "if we would slow down growth, there would be potential there". Other operating expenses (about 14% of sales): H1 2026 showed a 9.5% cut is possible (travel, external services, IT). Materials and external services (23% to 26%): variable with volume; in-sourcing reduced material intensity.

Modelling implication: In a downside case, assume management cuts CHF 10m to 15m of discretionary cost in the second year of a downturn, not the first.

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C-5213th of 27

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: SKAN has no cost programme, and none should be modelled as absolute cost reduction. What will reach the line items is efficiency in the form of costs growing more slowly than revenue: about CHF 8m to 12m of EBITDA by 2028 relative to a path on which the cost base grows in line with sales, worth about 2 points of margin, phased roughly one-third in 2026 (already visible in H1 2026), one-third in 2027 and one-third in 2028. This is already in my base case, not added to it. A formal programme under the new chief executive (the Mettler-Toledo model of pricing and productivity) would be genuine upside of a further 1 to 2 margin points by 2029, but it is unannounced, unquantified and would carry implementation costs, so I treat it as a bull-case lever with a first possible signal at the FY 2026 results on 23 March 2027.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • What is cuttable (AlphaSense operating-leverage report and the first set's C-77): other operating expenses (travel, external services, IT) fell 9.5% to CHF 23.6m in H1 2026, about CHF 2.5m a half-year; temporary staff (139 in 2023) and overtime flex; strategic-initiative and R&D spending (8.3% of sales in 2024, about CHF 25m to 30m) is partly deferrable, but management chose not to cut it in 2025 and pre-produced standard isolators instead of laying off engineers. The core engineering workforce is not cuttable without damaging delivery: the Stevanato precedent (an engineering reorganisation that took longer and cost more than planned, and lost key sales and engineering leaders) shows the risk.
  • Where efficiency comes from in practice: standardisation (VARIOSYS modular isolators; orders now roughly half large custom systems and half smaller standard isolators, which smooths factory loading and reduces engineering hours), in-sourcing of steelwork and assembly at Görlitz and Stein (material intensity down from about 27.6% toward 24% to 25%), decentralised hiring in lower-cost Germany, Slovenia and France rather than Allschwil (977 of 1,755 staff are in Switzerland), and service productivity tools (AI service tool, VR mock-ups replacing physical mock-ups). None is quantified by the company.
  • The new chief executive: Jonas Greutert (from 1-Jan-26; five years on Mettler-Toledo's executive board) introduced segment accountability and "operational excellence" but attached no savings target. His first-year evidence is consistent with discipline (operating expenses +13.8% against constant-currency sales +24.0% in H1 2026), but one half-year proves little. Peer precedent for what a structured programme delivers: Tecan targets two-thirds of a 400 basis point margin uplift from cost-out and booked CHF 18m of savings in H1 2026, but also CHF 7.6m of implementation costs; ATS (Comecer's parent) targets USD 75m of savings and 250 basis points. A SKAN programme, if announced, would more likely be in the 100 to 200 basis point range over three years given its smaller, labour-intensive base.
  • When: 2026 savings are already in H1; 2027 is the first full year under segment accountability; 2028 is when standardisation and Görlitz insourcing should be fully in the run rate. If management announces a programme in March 2027, its benefits would reach 2028 to 2029, and implementation costs would land in 2027.
  • The offset that will consume part of it: wage inflation of 2% to 3% a year in Switzerland, PAS operating staff before revenue, US service and assembly build-out, and the franc's effect on the Swiss cost base relative to euro and dollar revenue. My judgement is that efficiency roughly neutralises these frictions in 2026 to 2027 and adds about half a point a year of net margin in 2028 to 2030.

Line-item implications: no restructuring costs; other operating expenses growing about 5% a year against revenue growth of 9% to 13%; personnel growth of about 7% to 9% a year (headcount about 3% to 5%, wages 2% to 3%, plus PAS and US staff). In the downside case (a 2025-type year) I would assume management cuts CHF 10m to 15m of discretionary cost in the second year of the downturn, not the first.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4756P-0554
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?D5621st of 41full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C549th of 27

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's heading After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: Two loops will shape SKAN's financial lines from 2027 to 2030, and they act on different lines. The installed-base and consumables loop is reinforcing, well evidenced and steady: it will compound S&C revenue at 10% to 12% a year organically and lift S&C from 35% of revenue (2025) to about 44% (2030), but it will not by itself lift the S&C margin back to 2024 levels. The equipment-cycle loop (customer capex, conversion timing, under-absorption and advances) is balancing and lumpy: it decides the EBITDA margin, the EPS and above all the cash flow in any given year, and it is the loop most likely to turn against SKAN in 2028 to 2029 when pharma capex plateaus. Two other loops matter at the margin: the turnkey share loop runs against SKAN in standard equipment and, with a lag, slows the installed-base loop; the PAS adoption loop is unproven and is the only one that could change the margin structure. My base case assumes the reinforcing service loop wins on revenue mix and the balancing equipment loop slows revenue growth to about 9% in 2029.

The loops, how each shapes the lines:

  1. Installed-base annuity (reinforcing, strong, persistent). Each isolator delivered adds 15 or more years of mandatory requalification, retrofits and spare parts; the base is over 1,100 isolators and grows by several dozen a year; the service backlog nearly doubled to CHF 79.8m (mid-2026); organic S&C growth was 14.8% in H1 2026. Peers confirm the loop's durability: Getinge's recurring share rose to 67% from 60% (2023) and its sterile-transfer consumables kept double-digit growth through weak biopharma capex; Sartorius earns about 75% of Bioprocess revenue from validated single-use products. Its limits: SKAN does not disclose revenue per installed isolator; S&C was flat in H1 2025 when customers postponed maintenance and retrofits, so even this loop has timing noise. Financial effect: S&C organic growth of 12% (2027), 11% (2028) and 10% (2029 to 2030) before PAS; S&C share 38% (2026), 39% (2027), 41% (2028), 43% (2029), 44% (2030).
  2. The equipment-cycle loop (balancing, strong, cyclical). Orders lead E&S revenue by 12 to 36 months; customers now place firm orders 3 to 6 months before delivery against 12 months before the pandemic (Deutsche Bank, 15-Oct-26), and can postpone acceptance. A volume dip turns into a margin collapse (65% to 70% decremental), which management absorbs rather than cuts, and into a cash outflow as advances shrink. The 2027 large-pharma capex peak (Deutsche Bank: USD 58.87bn, then -1.3% in 2028), contract-manufacturer spare capacity (Fujifilm deferred Denmark phase two beyond 2028) and Stevanato's GLP-1 reset would show in SKAN's orders in 2027 and in revenue in 2028 to 2029. Against this, US onshoring equipment orders are expected mainly in H2 2027 (Sartorius via Bernstein) and Annex 1 upgrades continue; these partly fill the gap. Financial effect: E&S growth slowing from 11% (2026 and 2027) to 8% (2028) and 5% (2029), then 7% (2030).
  3. The turnkey share loop (reinforcing against SKAN, medium strength). Each filling line that a filler maker delivers with its own isolator builds that maker's validation record and makes its next turnkey bid more credible; an Optima manager puts SKAN's process-isolator share at about 50% from about 70% in two years. SKAN's counter-evidence (most large US fill-finish projects won in H1 2026, the Groninger "integra" partnership with Merz Pharma and Jubilant HollisterStier) shows it holds the complex end. Financial effect: a 1 to 2 point drag on E&S growth a year in standard lines (inside my E&S forecasts), and, with a lag of two to five years, fewer new isolators feeding loop 1. This is the link the market may miss: the service annuity is fed by equipment share, so an equipment share loss today is a service growth loss in 2029 to 2032.
  4. The advances loop (reinforcing in an upswing, balancing in a downswing). Rising orders bring advances (about 30% of order value), which fund working capital and capex. In 2025, about CHF 39m of the CHF 64.1m operating cash flow came from advances. Financial effect: my free cash flow assumes advances keep rising with backlog through 2027 (company-basis working capital at about -8% of sales) and flatten as orders plateau in 2028 (-7%, then -6.5%); a fall in orders would remove CHF 10m to 15m a year from operating cash flow before it touched EBITDA.
  5. Regulatory embedding (reinforcing, slow). SKAN trained about 700 inspectors for the European Commission (2024), hosted GMP inspector training at Allschwil (October 2025) and co-drafts ISPE containment guidance. It supports specification of SKAN in complex lines and protects the premium end. No direct line-item effect; it underpins my assumption that E&S share erosion is confined to standard lines.
  6. PAS adoption (potentially reinforcing, unproven). Early customers running stability batches on pre-qualified lines would create references and consume closed vials, which should attract more customers. But the loop has not started: Swissmedic approval slipped from end-2025 to Q1 2026 to "within 2026", and non-GMP runs began only in H1 2026. Financial effect: PAS revenue of 5 (2027), 14 (2028), 24 (2029) and 32 (2030), against management's about 50 at full capacity three years from commercial start; PAS EBITDA turning positive in 2027 and reaching about 40% by 2029 to 2030.

Interplay and the decisive question: The installed-base loop is strong enough to lift S&C revenue but not to offset an E&S downturn: at 2027 to 2028 scale, S&C EBITDA grows by about CHF 6m to 8m a year, while a 10% E&S volume shortfall costs CHF 15m to 18m. The equipment loop therefore still decides EPS through 2029, and the mix shift reduces, but does not remove, the cyclicality. Getinge took about a decade to move from 60% to 67% recurring; SKAN moves from 35% to 44% in five years in my base case, partly by acquisition.

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

F4038th of 41

Layer 1 · Guidance

The Investment Analyst's heading Other material or uncommon features of guidance and investor relations

Judgement: Five features matter. (1) Reporting is semi-annual, so the market sees the critical second half only in late March, and SKAN has used an unscheduled profit warning (6 November 2025) when needed. (2) Sell-side coverage is thin: five houses with named analysts, of which FactSet shows one contributor; consensus is effectively one model. (3) Disclosure gaps: no price/volume split, no currency sensitivity tables, hedges disclosed only off-balance sheet under Swiss GAAP FER, and backlog presented in several ways that confuse even professional readers. (4) The accounting basis (Swiss GAAP FER, goodwill offset against equity) makes peer comparison with IFRS companies harder. (5) The insider-trading proceedings concern how price-sensitive information was handled in 2023; whatever the appeal outcome, they reflect on information controls at the time.

Modelling implication: Expect discontinuous news flow (two results dates a year, plus occasional ad hoc releases) and treat any intra-year silence as uninformative.

6c

Case simplicity

Judgement43C-7950D-0260D-0336D-0434D-0542D-0656
Group judgement

Risks

F439th of 9

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The forecasts for the next 18 months are a well-founded statement of where SKAN is going and a weak statement of when it gets there. The earlier sets fit together (D-01), and where they do not, the corrections mostly help: the cost base has been flat for five half-years, the 2026 segment build already implied a higher margin than the input, and the 2026 and 2027 forecasts ask nothing of the business that it has not done before, since 2027 is in essence 2024's equipment year repeated on a larger services base and a leaner cost base (D-02). PAS, the one capability SKAN has not shown, matters only from 2028. Against that, the change in profit over 18 months comes from close to a single driver that customers control (D-03); the cone for 2027 EPS runs from about 0.96 to about 2.10 around 1.74, with a probability-weighted value of about 1.63 against a FactSet figure of 1.84 (D-04); the company reports the decisive half once, three months after it ends, and gives no backlog phasing (D-05); the chain from sales to EPS is clean but the link from backlog to sales is not observable, and the observable external variables bear on 2028 and 2029, not on 2027 (D-06); and the multiple, mainly SKAN's own, owes perhaps a third to a half of its recent rise to a sector tide (D-07).

My steer to the Consumer has three parts. First, state the base case as a level, not a year: about CHF 70m of EBITDA on about CHF 435m of sales, with high confidence in the level and moderate confidence that it is 2027. Second, move the emphasis of the risk from margin to timing: on a flat cost base the margin skew is, if anything, upward at given sales, and the whole downward skew is the ease with which one customer can move one project by a year. Third, treat the next two company dates (preliminary figures, 28 January 2027 if confirmed, and the results of 23 March 2027) as the points at which about a third of the 2027 uncertainty is removed: the opening backlog, the second-half margin and year-end headcount will show whether both the level and the leaner cost base are intact. Until then the case is low in predictability by the standard of an 18-month forecast and moderate by the standard of a three-year one.

C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

D5025th of 41

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Narrow in product (isolators and their aftermarket), customers (top five clients about 43% of order intake in 2020; 81% biopharma) and production footprint (Switzerland, Germany, Belgium, France, Slovenia, a small US base), but spread across three sales regions (Europe 59%, Americas 25% to 28%, Asia 12% to 14%). The narrowness is not the problem for predictability; project lumpiness is. The perimeter is widening (consumables, software, PAS, US) which adds complexity and minorities but, by raising the recurring share, should gradually improve predictability.

Reasoning: One delayed customer moves the year; one large order moves the half-year. The S&C share rising toward 50% is the main structural improvement in predictability; it is partly offset by Metronik's project-based software integration, which brings some lumpiness into S&C.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

D605th of 9

Layer 3 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does past and present delivery make the expected change plausible, and does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has?

Judgement: For the next 18 months the expected change is highly plausible as an end-state, because it asks the business to return to a place it has already been, not to become something new. The 2027 forecast is 2024 revisited on a larger services base: E&S is asked to earn on 267 of sales what it earned on 271 in 2024 (a margin of about 11.5% against 11.2%), and S&C is asked to earn six to seven points less margin than in 2024 (about 23% against 29.5%) on 87% more sales. Every strength this needs has been demonstrated: winning orders, earning 17.5% to 18.9% in the second half whenever projects flow (three years running, including 2025), growing installed-base revenue, and, new in 2026, holding the cost base flat. What the 18-month forecast needs that SKAN has not demonstrated is not a strength it could acquire but a condition it does not control: customers taking delivery on schedule. Beyond 2027 the picture changes: about 40% of the increase in EBITDA between 2027 and 2030 comes from PAS, a regulated manufacturing service that SKAN has never run, that no equipment maker has launched before, and for which no contract has been disclosed. So the thesis rests on demonstrated strengths for 18 months and leans on an unproven capability for years three and four.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • The arithmetic of the change (my inputs). EBITDA rises 31 between 2025 and 2027 (38.6 to 69.8). About 21 is E&S returning from 9.4 to about 30.5, the level it earned in 2024 (30.3) on the same sales; about 10 is S&C growing from 29.1 to about 39 with seven extra months of the acquired businesses and 12% organic growth, at a margin below each of the last three full years; PAS contributes about nil. None of it needs price, share gain or a recovery of the S&C margin to its 2024 level.
  • Demonstrated, with the evidence. Orders: record backlog of 442.3, order intake +16.3% in H1 2026, most of the larger United States fill-finish projects won. Margin when volume arrives: second-half margins of 17.5%, 17.9% and 18.9% in 2023 to 2025; full-year margins above the top of the guided range in 2023 and 2024. Installed base: organic S&C sales +14.8% in H1 2026, S&C order intake of 81.2 (+54.6%) and a service backlog of 79.8 (+50% in six months). Cost control: finding A1.
  • Not demonstrated. (1) Conversion on schedule: sales guidance was met in 2022 (a weak first half made good in the second) and 2023, and missed in 2024 and 2025; two in four. The cause each time was customers' readiness, and no reorganisation at SKAN changes that; segment accountability may shorten SKAN's own part of a project but the AlphaSense report rightly calls its effect unproven. (2) Running a GMP filling service: Swissmedic certification has slipped three times, partly on SKAN's own software validation; in March 2025 management put the probability of on-time approval at 85% and then missed, which tells the Consumer how much weight to put on management's probabilities for events a regulator controls. Precedents for new sterile lines run by experienced contract manufacturers with anchor customers are three to four years to good utilisation (Jubilant's Spokane line), and Stevanato's Fishers plant took about five years from plan to gross-profit break-even; usable capacity is capped at 225 to 230 sterile days a year; contract fillers typically earn 18% to 26% EBITDA margins, with Rovi's sterile-only business (about 50%) the single support for SKAN's "up to 50%". The second set's ramp (5, 14, 24, 32 of revenue; about 40% margin) is at the favourable end of these precedents for an operator with no disclosed customers. (3) Defending the S&C margin: it has fallen for 18 months; the AlphaSense report could not split the causes (acquisition mix, PAS pre-operating costs carried in the segment, service headcount, the absence of 2024's exceptional closed-vial order) because SKAN discloses none of them. The forecast does not need a recovery to 2024 levels, only that the fall stops at about 22% to 23%.
  • Peer precedents after similar slumps. Recovery came on time where the service share was large and the cost base was held (Syntegon grew EBITDA 27% in 2025; GEA converts more than 80% of its backlog within twelve months with services at 40% of sales) and slipped where custom engineering had to be reorganised (Stevanato's Engineering segment is not expected back to normal profitability until mid-2027, several quarters after its plan). SKAN sits between: S&C is 38% of sales, but its E&S backlog converts only 55% to 65% within twelve months, so it is longer-dated and more exposed to customers' timing than GEA's.
  • The one newly acquired strength. Holding costs flat while sales grow is a change in revealed behaviour and is what turns a 2024-type year into a better-than-2024 margin. It has been shown for one half-year under the new chief executive (and, by necessity, through 2025). The second half of 2026 is its first test with volume.

Implication: lean on the end-state, not on the year. The base case deserves high confidence that SKAN earns about CHF 70m of EBITDA on about CHF 435m of sales; it deserves only moderate confidence that this is the calendar year 2027.

D-03In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?F369th of 9full answer under 2a Internal risks
D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

F349th of 9

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The cone is narrow for 2026 and wide for 2027, and it is skewed down, but for a different reason from the one the earlier sets give. For 2026 I put attributable EPS at 1.20 to 1.57 around a base of 1.38 (about 14% either side), with half the year reported and no warning issued. For 2027 I put it at about 0.96 to 2.10 around a base of 1.74: 45% below to 21% above, with a probability-weighted value of about 1.63, which is 6% below my base and 11% below the FactSet figure of 1.84. The earlier sets attribute the skew to an asymmetric margin response (a shortfall costs 60% to 70% at the margin while a beat earns 25% to 40%). With the cost base now flat in both directions (finding A1), a beat should drop through at 50% to 70% as well, as it did in H1 2026 (48% including the acquired cost base). The skew now comes from the revenue distribution itself: a customer can move a project a year later with one decision, and almost nothing can bring a project a year earlier. So the direction of the case is predictable and its end-state is well supported; the calendar year in which a given level of profit lands is not, and SKAN's EPS for a year cannot be known to better than about 20% until roughly two months before that year ends. That is low predictability for an 18-month forecast, even though it is a reasonably predictable business over three years.

The cone for 2027 (my scenarios; the second set's, re-run on my inputs):

2027 Bear (15%) Mild miss (20%) Base (45%) Bull (20%) Probability-weighted FactSet figure
Sales 405 420 436 455 432 454
EBITDA margin 12.0% 14.5% 16.0% 17.5% 15.5% 15.4%
EBITDA 48.6 60.9 69.8 79.6 66.8 70.1
Attributable EPS 0.96 1.41 1.74 2.10 1.63 1.84

Bear: a customer-controlled postponement of 2025's size, or an early turn in customer capital spending. Mild miss: one large project slips into 2028, or the CHF 15m to 20m item is cancelled with nothing to replace it. Bull: conversion on schedule, the cost base held, PAS certified early in 2027. The FactSet EPS figure sits between my base and my bull case, as the second set said; the FactSet EBITDA figure now sits on my base.

Reasoning and critical evidence:

  • Why the cone is this wide. About 61% of 2027 sales is E&S, recognised by percentage of completion with 75% to 80% of a project's value in phases the customer's site readiness governs; non-material costs are fixed within the year; so CHF 10m of sales is worth CHF 6.5m to 7m of EBITDA and about CHF 0.24 of EPS (14% of 2027 EPS) in either direction. SKAN's own record sets the base rate: the sales guide was met in two of the last four years (2022 and 2023) and missed in two (by about four points of growth in 2024, by more than twenty in 2025).
  • Why it stays wide until late. About 73% of 2026 EBITDA and about 66% of 2027 EBITDA fall in the second half. At each August half-year report two-thirds of the year's profit is still ahead, and SKAN reports nothing between August and the following January or March unless it has to warn. The H1 2027 report (August 2027) will therefore narrow the 2027 cone only modestly; the full narrowing comes with the preliminary figures in January 2028.
  • What narrows it, and when. The preliminary 2026 figures (28 January 2027 if finding A4 is right) fix 2026 sales and, more importantly, second-half order intake and the opening backlog for 2027; the full results on 23 March 2027 fix the second-half margin, the cost base, year-end advances and the first 2027 guide. I expect these two events together to remove about a third of the 2027 cone: they will show whether the opening E&S backlog supports E&S sales of about 267 at a 55% to 65% conversion, and whether the cost base is still flat. Thresholds (the second set's, which I keep): second-half order intake above 220 or below 180; 2026 sales above 392 or below 384; and, on my inputs, a second-half EBITDA margin above 19.2% (which would put the year above the guided range) or below 17%.
  • The skew in margin is, if anything, upward; the skew in revenue is downward. My base margins (14.5% and 16.0%) sit in the lower half of what a flat cost base would produce at base-case sales, so a surprise on margin at given sales is more likely to be positive. A surprise on the timing of sales is more likely to be negative. For EPS the second dominates because of the size of the revenue tail.
  • Automatic stabilisers are small. No bonus was paid for 2025, which cushioned the second half of that year by a few million; management did not cut staff in 2025 and would not in the first year of a shortfall (the second set's C-77 and P-03 judgement, which I share). There is little left to flex on the way down precisely because the cost base has already been held flat.
  • The share-price cone has the same shape. At the guideline fair multiple on my inputs the 18-month value is about CHF 81 to 82 (+16% to +18%); the model's first update shows CHF 86.89 (+26%). The first set's troughs stand: about CHF 55 to 57 (-20%) for a de-rating with estimates intact and about CHF 40 to 45 (-36% to -43%) for a moderate miss. An upside of about a fifth against a plausible downside of a fifth to two-fifths is the price expression of a cone that is skewed down.
D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

F428th of 9

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: Helpful in content, unhelpful in frequency, and missing the one disclosure the thesis most needs. Twice a year SKAN gives more than most companies of its size: order intake by segment and region, backlog split between equipment and service, segment EBITDA that sums to the group, headcount, customer advances, and candid statements of what is at risk (the CHF 15m to 20m item, kept visible for a year and described in August 2026 as "unsolved but constructive"). But it reports only half-yearly, gives no backlog phasing (how much of the 442 is scheduled for which year, and how much is in design against manufacturing), and discloses nothing that would let an outsider follow PAS (certification date, customers, costs) or decompose the S&C margin. Over the next six months the Consumer will get one and a half hard readings: preliminary sales and orders (28 January 2027, to be verified) and the full results with the 2027 guide (23 March 2027). Between now and late January the only company signal is the absence of a warning. That is adequate for confirming or breaking the 2026 leg of the thesis and for resetting the 2027 cone, and poor for anything in between.

What the disclosure lets the Consumer monitor in the next six months, with the readings that matter:

  • By 31 December 2026: Swissmedic certification of the PAS facility. The company's stated aim is "within 2026". No announcement by year-end is a fourth slip; the base case (second set) already assumes H1 2027, so a slip is in the numbers and a certification before the March results would be a positive surprise.
  • Until late January: silence. After 6 November, when last year's warning came, each further week without an ad hoc release modestly lowers the probability of a large miss for 2026. It says nothing about a year-end slippage of the 2024 kind.
  • 28 January 2027 (to be verified): preliminary 2026 figures. Sales (base 387; guidance read plainly needs 390 to 397), second-half order intake (base about 200; the Americas share and whether United States projects continued to convert), and the year-end backlog (base about 420). Margin is unlikely to be given at this point, so the reading on the cost base waits for March.
  • Late January to mid-March 2027: peers. Danaher, Thermo Fisher and Repligen (equipment orders; Repligen's were at a five-year high in Q3 2026), Sartorius on 23 February (equipment recovery and its 2027 outlook), Stevanato (GLP-1 volumes and Engineering), Getinge (sterile transfer and whether pharmaceutical customers still delay large equipment decisions), Tecan in mid-March. These inform 2027 orders and 2028 revenue more than the 2027 result.
  • 23 March 2027: full results and the first 2027 guide. Second-half EBITDA margin (my base 18.4%; the three prior second halves were 17.5% to 18.9%), the E&S second-half margin (15% or better) and the S&C second-half margin (23.5% or better, which would show the first-half dip was timing), year-end headcount (at or below about 1,700 confirms discipline; a return toward 1,755 would not), customer advances (132.2 at mid-year; about 126 in my base), free cash flow (about 14 to 15), the fate of the item at risk, the form and level of the 2027 guide, and any statement on PAS customers.
  • 28 April 2027 (to be verified): annual general meeting. The first shareholder vote since the chief financial officer's conviction; proxy advisers' recommendations on the discharge of the board will be published a few weeks before. No investor, proxy adviser or analyst has yet reacted in public (the AlphaSense report found none between 17 and 25 November).

What is missing, and why it matters:

  • Backlog phasing and stage. GEA tells investors that more than 80% of its backlog converts within twelve months; SKAN gives no such figure, and its own conversion (55% to 65% of opening E&S backlog, by the first set's derivation) has to be inferred. The H1 2026 book-to-bill of 1.5 included engineering contracts and slot reservations converted into full orders, which are early-stage and convert late; the disclosure does not let the Consumer size this.
  • The S&C margin. No split between service, consumables, software and PAS pre-operating costs; no standalone figures for Metronik, ABC Transfer or Aseptic Technologies. The most debated number in the case cannot be decomposed from outside.
  • PAS. No certification date, no customers, no pricing, no depreciation start, no asset lives.
  • Price against volume, currency sensitivities and hedges, segment guidance, a dated capital spending plan. The chief financial officer's remark in March 2026 that capital spending would stay at "mid-single-digit levels" (as cited in the AlphaSense report) is ambiguous against 13.7 spent in H1 2026 and the second set's 30 for the year.
  • Frequency. Listed peers report quarterly; SKAN's thesis-critical half (the second) is seen once, three months after it ends.

A point for the memo. The disclosure is good enough that the thesis cannot drift unnoticed for long: order intake, backlog, segment EBITDA, headcount and advances together reveal each of the three drivers twice a year. Its weakness is that when information does arrive it arrives all at once, so the share price adjusts in steps (the 2025 warning; the H1 2026 results), not gradually.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

D566th of 9

Layer 3 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into operational drivers, onto revenue, cost and margin lines, and through to EPS; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to EPS?

Judgement: The chain is unusually clean from revenue down to EPS and opaque at exactly one link, the timing of revenue; and the external chain is clear in direction but so slow that it tells the Consumer about 2028 and 2029, not about the next 18 months. Given half-year sales, SKAN's EPS can be computed from outside with few assumptions: materials at about a quarter of sales, a non-material cost base of about CHF 115m per half-year that has not moved organically for two and a half years, depreciation, small financial items, tax of about 17% and minorities of about 3% to 4%, with no adjusted-profit layer, no restructuring charges, low debt and a flat share count. The company's guidance (sales growth and an EBITDA margin) therefore maps onto EPS almost mechanically. What does not trace is how the company gets from a backlog of 442 to the sales of a given half-year, and how its mid-term ambitions (S&C at half of sales, PAS at CHF 50m of revenue, "triple sales, quadruple EBITDA") map onto any dated line.

Internal transmission, link by link:

  • Backlog to sales: the weak link. Stated: the backlog, its split, and that manufacturing and assembly carry 75% to 80% of a project's value under cost-to-cost percentage of completion. Not stated: which projects reach those phases in which half. Customers control the answer. This single link carries most of the uncertainty in D-03 and D-04.
  • Sales to EBITDA: clear. Segment EBITDA is disclosed and sums to the group; personnel cost and headcount are disclosed; the cost base is stable enough to treat as a constant plus materials. On my figures EBITDA is approximately sales times 0.74 to 0.75, less about 230 a year in 2026. The second-half uplift in margin is a volume and mix effect, not a seasonal change in costs (non-material costs were the same in the first and second halves of 2024).
  • EBITDA to EPS: clear and short. Attributable net income is 54% to 56% of EBITDA in 2026 and 2027; reported EPS is the brokers' EPS (2025: 0.724 against 0.720). The only moving part is the step-up in depreciation when the PAS facility goes into service, whose date and size are undisclosed (about CHF 5m to 6.5m a year; worth about CHF 0.04 to 0.06 of EPS on the difference).
  • Orders to cash: clear. Customers pay about 30% on order, so operating cash flow is roughly EBITDA less tax plus the change in advances; advances and backlog are both disclosed. This link is why cash turns before profit in either direction (operating cash flow of 8.7 in 2023 when orders fell 28%; 64.1 in 2025 when advances rose 39).
  • Mid-term ambitions to lines: poor. The PAS target has no ramp, no customers and no price; the 50/50 mix has no date and is being approached partly by acquisition; the "triple and quadruple" aspiration has no date at all and was presented by the chief financial officer now under conviction. They cannot be tested against a line in any year, which is why I treat them, with the second set, as the bull case.

External transmission (value chain to EPS), with its lags:

  • Regulation (Annex 1, United States contamination-control expectations) and drug pipelines (antibody-drug conjugates, biosimilars, GLP-1) set customers' capital budgets; budgets are released late in the third quarter and orders placed in the fourth (Optima manager, 29-Jul-25); an order brings cash at once (advances) and revenue over 12 to 36 months; a commissioned isolator brings service revenue a year or two later for 15 years or more. Customers' capital spending therefore reaches SKAN's orders within two to four quarters, its cash at the same time, its revenue in one to three years, and its service revenue after that.
  • The practical consequence for an 18-month forecast: the observable external variables (large-pharma and contract-manufacturer capital spending, peers' equipment orders, biotech funding, the franc through tender competitiveness) bear on 2027 orders and so on 2028 and 2029 revenue. United States onshoring orders are running nine to twelve months late, with deliveries from late 2027 into 2028 (Repligen through Bernstein, 18-Nov-26); a slowing of large-biopharma capital spending growth to low single digits in 2027 (Deutsche Bank, 15-Oct-26) and of contract-manufacturer capacity growth in 2026 to 2028 (Bernstein, 18-Nov-26) would show in SKAN's revenue in 2028 and 2029. The external variables that decide 2027 EPS are specific to a few customers (a site not ready, engineers not available, one GLP-1 maker's ramp) and are not publicly observable.
  • So the external chain is a good guide to whether the second set's slower growth from 2028 is right, and a poor guide to whether 2027 lands on time. The one fast external channel is sentiment, not earnings: a peer's order comment moves the share price the same week (Sartorius fell about 10% in July 2026 on Danaher's delayed resin orders).

C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

F3432nd of 41

Layer 1 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading Perception- or sentiment-driven sudden risks

Judgement: SKAN is unusually exposed to sentiment shocks because it is small (market capitalisation about CHF 1.56bn), illiquid (average daily value traded about USD 3m to 5m; about 38,000 shares a day), thinly covered (one broker visible in FactSet), at the top of its sector on relative strength after a 48% twelve-month rise, and carrying a governance headline.

The main triggers:

  • Sector read-across on equipment orders: Danaher's delayed resin orders cut Sartorius by about 10% in July 2026; Tecan sold off on flat Q2 orders despite an earnings beat. Danaher, Thermo Fisher and Repligen report in late January to February 2027 and Sartorius on 23 February 2027, all before SKAN's results on 23 March 2027.
  • GLP-1 and pharma capex narrative: Stevanato's 2027 reset, a 2028 capex plateau (Deutsche Bank), US drug-pricing headlines.
  • Governance headlines: further reporting on the CFO case; ESG-screened holders reducing positions.
  • Market regime: the Fitness for Fighting digest reads risk-off beneath an index at its high, with Fragility (small, complex stocks) at Double Underweight and Momentum Underweight; the healthcare rotation is judged supportive for three months and fading to neutral within twelve. A reversal of the defensive rotation, or a return of the discount-rate squeeze, would hit an illiquid small cap at 38x next year's earnings hardest.
  • Single-broker risk: one downgrade or estimate cut moves "consensus" entirely.

C-61How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?F3641st of 41full answer under 4b Management quality
C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

F3040th of 41

Layer 1 · Guidance

The Investment Analyst's heading Record, nature and behaviour in upgrading guidance

Judgement: SKAN has no record of upgrading guidance during a year; its pattern is to set an ambitious range at the full-year results, confirm it at the half-year, and warn late if needed. In 2025 it raised its margin guide at the start of the year (14% to 16%, from 13% to 15% for 2024), confirmed guidance in August 2025, and issued a profit warning on 6 November 2025. In 2024 it missed the sales guide while beating the margin range. The new chief executive has no record yet; his first act was to confirm, not raise, 2026 guidance despite strong orders.

Reasoning: The predecessor's guidance behaviour was optimistic on revenue timing. The new chief executive, from a culture that values reliability, has a motive to reset to a beatable 2027 bar; that would be a change in behaviour worth watching on 23 March 2027.

C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

F3539th of 41

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

The Investment Analyst's heading Record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and estimates over one, three and five years, including comparable conditions

Judgement: Weak over one year, mixed over three, better over five, and the pattern is consistent: SKAN meets or beats on margin when projects flow and misses on revenue when customers delay. One year: 2025 was a large miss (sales -7.7% against mid-teens growth guided; EBITDA margin 11.6% against 14% to 16%), and FY 2025 consensus EPS fell 51% over twelve months (from as high as CHF 2.14 to 0.72). H1 2026 exceeded depressed expectations on sales and confirmed guidance. Three years: 2023 met sales guidance (15.5% against 15% to 19%) and beat on margin (15.7% against 13% to 15%); 2024 missed sales (12.9% against mid-to-upper teens) and beat on margin (15.8% against 13% to 15%); 2025 missed both. Five years: the pre-2023 period was one of rapid growth (2019 to 2023 sales CAGR about 19%) but the files hold little detail on guidance in 2021 and 2022.

Comparable conditions: The closest analogue to the coming period is 2024: a strong order year after a slump, with a large backlog, in which about 30 projects slipped at year-end because customers were not ready, and sales missed guidance. The 2026 set-up (record backlog, recovering orders, H2-heavy plan, cautious customers) resembles 2024 more than 2023. That argues for leaning revenue forecasts slightly below the top of guidance and allowing for slippage into the following year rather than outright loss.

P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

F4023rd of 27

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: The forecasts deserve high weight for 2026 (half the year is reported and the second half is backlog conversion), moderate weight for 2027, and low weight for 2028 onward. They should be leaned later on timing, not lower on end-states: SKAN's record is to deliver what it controls (capacity, acquisitions, cost discipline in good years, margins when projects flow) and to over-promise on what customers and regulators control (revenue conversion, PAS approval). I have therefore leaned revenue below consensus (by about 2% in 2026, 4% in 2027 and 8% in 2028) and PAS later (first revenue 2027, about 60% of management's full-capacity level by 2030), while keeping the margin path in line with consensus, because SKAN has historically met or beaten its margin ranges whenever volume arrived (2023 and 2024). The direction of the lean is down on EPS and cash timing; the size of the lean is modest because the backlog (CHF 442.3m, 1.22x trailing sales) is real.

The record, read for what it says about each line:

  • Revenue: 2023 met (+15.5% against 15% to 19% guided); 2024 missed (+12.9% against mid-to-upper teens), with about 30 projects slipping because customers were not ready; 2025 missed badly (-7.7% against mid-teens growth), with guidance confirmed in August 2025 and withdrawn on 6 November 2025. The 2026 set-up (record backlog after a slump, H2-heavy plan, cautious customers, a CHF 15m to 20m item at risk of cancellation and about CHF 40m of delayed backlog) resembles 2024 more than 2023. Lean: H2 2026 sales of about 222.5 (+12%) rather than the 228 to 232 that the guidance midpoint and consensus need; full-year growth 16.1%, the bottom of "high teens". I put roughly a 30% chance on full-year growth below 15%.
  • Margin: beaten in 2023 (15.7% against 13% to 15%) and 2024 (15.8% against 13% to 15%), missed in 2025 (11.6% against 14% to 16%). When volume arrives, SKAN's H2 margin has been 17.5% to 18.9%. Lean: none; I use 17.6% for H2 2026, giving 14.1% for the year, the middle of 13% to 15%.
  • PAS: three slips (end-2025, Q1 2026, "within 2026"), partly caused by SKAN's own software validation. Lean: Swissmedic approval in H1 2027, first revenue in 2027, a ramp slower than management's three years. Each CHF 10m of PAS revenue is worth about CHF 5m of EBITDA and CHF 0.18 of EPS, so the PAS timing alone moves 2028 EPS by up to 10%.
  • Peers in the same conditions: Syntegon grew EBITDA 27% in 2025 while SKAN's fell 32%; Stevanato and Tecan both reset expectations during 2024 to 2026; the sector's rule is that lumpy project companies are punished for misses. The record does not argue that SKAN is a weaker business than these peers, but that its forecasts carry wider error bars.
  • The new chief executive has no completed guidance cycle; he confirmed rather than raised 2026 guidance despite H1 orders up 16.3%. His first full-year guide on 23 March 2027 is the event that will tell us whether the guiding style has moved from ambitious to reliable; my working assumption is that he sets a beatable 2027 bar (low-to-mid-teens growth, margin "further improving" or a range around 15%), which is consistent with my numbers rather than with consensus.

How much weight, by year (my judgement of the cone around my base case):

  • 2026: revenue 380 to 395 (±2%), EBITDA 50 to 57, EPS 1.20 to 1.42. High weight.
  • 2027: revenue 405 to 460 (-7% to +5%), EBITDA 49 to 76, EPS 1.00 to 2.00. Moderate weight; the cone is skewed down because a postponement costs 60% to 70% at the margin while a beat earns 25% to 40%.
  • 2028 to 2030: revenue ±12% or more, EPS ±30%. Low weight; these years depend on the pharma capex cycle, the turnkey share trend and PAS, each of which is outside the backlog.

For the third set: the forecasts rest on one historical pattern (margin follows volume) and one assumption that has failed before (conversion on schedule); D-02 should test the second, not the first.

Financial forecasts

D4718th of 27

The financial model that follows from the first set's foundations is a recovery with a ceiling. Capacity, people and acquisitions built in 2023 to 2025 give SKAN strong operating leverage as the record backlog converts, and the move from heavy investment to normal capex turns a five-year history of negative free cash flow into roughly CHF 150m of cumulative free cash flow over 2026 to 2030. But the recovery mainly restores what 2025 lost: on my numbers EPS regains its 2024 level only in 2028, revenue growth from the 2024 peak averages about 8% a year rather than the mid-to-upper teens of the mid-term targets, and the margin reaches the low end of "upper teens" only by 2030. Consensus (one broker) is not wrong on margin, and its depreciation is already adequate; it is early on timing: it assumes on-schedule conversion in 2027, no capex plateau in 2028 and a faster PAS ramp, which puts its 2027 and 2028 EPS 8% and 13% above mine and close to my bull case. For the third set, predictability over 18 months rests on three concentrated drivers, each of which has slipped before: H2 2026 and 2027 E&S conversion, the PAS approval date and the S&C margin; the cone is wide (2027 EPS about 1.00 to 2.00) and skewed down because SKAN's cost base magnifies a revenue shortfall four to eight times. For the fourth set, the gap between my base case and consensus is about timing rather than thesis: a beatable 2027 guide on 23 March 2027 would most likely bring consensus revenue and EPS down toward my numbers, while the cash flow would surprise positively.

C-49What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?D5023rd of 41full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

D4619th of 27

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Below operating profit, SKAN's earnings are clean and the leakage is modest and stable: net financial items of CHF 1.5m to 3.5m, tax of 16.5% to 17.5%, minorities of about 4% of net income, no buybacks and a flat share count. These items take about 20% of pre-tax profit, against 17% to 25% in 2022 to 2025, so they neither help nor hurt EPS growth materially. What does change below EBITDA is depreciation: the PAS facility adds about CHF 5m a year from 2027, so D&A rises from 13.2 (2025) to 17.5 (2027) and 22.5 (2030), which is why EBIT and EPS grow more slowly than EBITDA in 2027. The biggest change versus the last five years is in free cash flow: with capex falling from 13% to 14% of sales (2024 to 2025) to 7% to 8%, free cash flow turns from a cumulative -40m (2021 to 2025, FactSet basis) to about +150m over 2026 to 2030, a conversion of about 65% of net income. Relative to consensus, below-EBITDA items explain about a third of my 2027 EPS gap (CHF 0.05 to 0.06 of 0.15), and almost none of it is depreciation.

Item by item:

  • Depreciation and amortisation: buildings 40 years, machinery and equipment 3 to 10 years, other assets 5 to 10 years. D&A barely moved (12.4, 12.5, 13.2 in 2023 to 2025) despite capex of 35.3, 53.8 and 45.9, which is consistent with the PAS facility (about CHF 70m) still being under construction or not yet in service. H1 2026 D&A was 6.6 (EBITDA 15.3 less EBIT 8.7). I assume PAS assets go into service around Q2 2027 at about CHF 5m a year (a mix of fit-out and machinery; the split is not disclosed; if the premises are leased, as the leased office space near headquarters suggests, more of the 70m is short-lived equipment and the charge could be CHF 6m to 7m). Path: 13.6, 17.5, 19.5, 21.0, 22.5. Consensus, read correctly (EBITDA minus EBIT), is 14.9, 18.4, 21.0: no gap.
  • Goodwill: under Swiss GAAP FER the CHF 86.8m of goodwill on Metronik (75.5) and ABC Transfer (11.3) was offset against equity; theoretical amortisation over five years was CHF 8.3m in 2025 and should be about CHF 17m to 18m a year in 2026 to 2029 (my estimate: 86.8 over five years plus legacy goodwill), which would cut reported net profit by about half if it were charged. Brokers' EPS is on the reported basis (2025: 0.720 against reported 0.724), so this does not affect the comparison with consensus; it matters for comparison with IFRS peers' reported (not adjusted) EPS and for return on capital.
  • Net financial items: see P-02; -3.5, -2.5, -2.0, -2.0, -1.5. Consensus is about CHF 1m a year less negative.
  • Tax: 14.1%, 15.0%, 14.8%, 12.8% and 17.8% in 2021 to 2025. The 2025 rise reflects a profit mix tilted away from low-tax Switzerland (the Swiss E&S business earned little) and fewer loss carryforwards. Pillar Two does not apply below EUR 750m of revenue. As Swiss E&S profits recover the rate should ease: 17.5% (2026), 17.0% (2027 to 2028), 16.5% (2029 to 2030) as PAS profits arise in Switzerland. Consensus implies about 15% to 18% depending on whether the broker deducts minorities.
  • Minorities: about 1.2 (2026), 1.4, 1.6, 1.8, then about 0.5 after an assumed 2029 purchase of Metronik's 24% (put and call from 2029 at fair value). ABC Transfer has no minority line. Aseptic Technologies' 10% remains.
  • Share count: 22.5m shares; share-based short- and long-term incentives from January 2026, settled in equity and covered by treasury purchases (CHF 0.3m in H1 2026). I model about 20,000 shares delivered a year and CHF 0.5m to 1.0m of treasury purchases: the count is flat. No buyback programme; none is likely before 2029.
  • One-offs: none expected in the base case. Watch items: a cancellation of the CHF 15m to 20m backlog item could bring a provision or write-down of work in progress; a failed CFO appeal could bring advisory and transition costs; a PAS impairment only in a refusal scenario.

EBITDA to net income conversion (my figures): net income attributable over EBITDA was 68% in 2024 and 42% in 2025; I forecast 54% (2026), 56% (2027), 59% (2028), 60% (2029) and 63% (2030). Consensus implies 56%, 59% and 62% for 2026 to 2028; the difference is my higher financial charges and slightly higher tax, worth about CHF 0.05 a share a year.

Free cash flow (my definition: operating cash flow less all capex, including intangibles, less lease payments): 14 (2026), 24 (2027), 26 (2028), 38 (2029), 48 (2030), against consensus 8, 18, 22 (FactSet's estimate). My figures are higher because I assume advances keep rising with the backlog through 2027 and capex of 30 to 38 (7% to 8% of sales); they would be 10 to 15 lower in any year in which orders fall. Capex of 30 in 2026 is consistent with 13.7 in H1 2026; the mid-term growth capex of CHF 100m to 120m guided in May 2025 is largely spent if the US assembly facility proceeds in 2027 to 2028, which I include at about CHF 4m to 8m a year in 2027 to 2028.

Scenarios for 2027 (to frame the third set's cone):

  • Bull (about 20%): conversion on schedule, PAS approved early in 2027, organic cost growth held near 6% to 8%: revenue about 455, EBITDA margin about 16.8%, EPS about 2.00.
  • Base (about 45%): as above: 436, 15.5%, 1.69.
  • Mild miss (about 20%): one large project slips into 2028 or the CHF 15m to 20m item is cancelled: revenue about 420, margin about 14.2%, EPS about 1.40.
  • Bear (about 15%): a 2025-type year (GLP-1 or vaccine postponements, early capex plateau): revenue about 405, margin about 12%, EPS about 1.00, and free cash flow near zero as advances fall.
  • The probability-weighted 2027 EPS is about 1.59, about 14% below consensus.

What would change my base case: up: an H2 2026 order intake above CHF 220m with US onshoring wins, a dated Swissmedic approval, an H1 2027 margin of 13% or more, or a quantified efficiency programme; down: H2 2026 intake below CHF 180m (cut 2027 growth toward 8% to 10%), confirmation of the CHF 15m to 20m cancellation together with further GLP-1 deferrals, another PAS slip beyond mid-2027, or a further fall in the S&C margin in H2 2026.

Margins/growth vs. past
